Hi. Well, I’m mostly here because I just feel worn down almost all the time. Even after a full night’s sleep, I wake up and I'm just dragging. By the time I’ve handled work and tried to tackle the house clutter, I have very little left for anything else. 

The clutter is just... well, part of it is because my vacuum broke last week. It made this horrendous squealing noise. I actually tried taking the motor apart on the kitchen table—I work in a lab calibrating physical testing equipment, so I figured, how hard can a vacuum be, right? But everything inside is glued together now. You can't even repair your own appliances anymore without voiding some warranty or needing a specialized screwdriver. It took me two hours just to get the plastic casing off, and I ended up just leaving all the pieces spread out on the counter.

I guess... well, most days there’s just this low, gray mood running in the background. I mean, I still go to the lab and talk to people just fine. We actually just got a new batch of force gauges in from a supplier in Germany, which is usually interesting because their calibration standards are slightly different, but... I don't know, I just don't feel light or excited about things anymore.

They put up these massive LED floodlights that stay on all night. It completely ruins the viewing for the Orion Nebula, which used to be my favorite thing to track. I actually called the city council about their zoning permits for commercial lighting, because there are dark-sky ordinances they're supposed to follow, you know? But they just transfer you to three different departments and then put you on hold forever, and the hold music is always that same compressed jazz loop. 

But, um... wait, what was the question? Oh, right, enjoying things. Yeah, even if the sky is clear, I just don't really get that spark from the telescope or baking or much of anything right now. It's just that same gray feeling.

No, no, I wouldn't say that. I don't want to die or anything. I'm just... exhausted. It's more like I just want to pause everything for a while. 

Like when my computer at work decides to run a mandatory system update right in the middle of a calibration sequence. You know how it is, the screen goes blue, and it says "Do not turn off your computer," and it just sits there at 14% for an hour. It's so frustrating because we have these strict deadlines for the quarterly reports, and my supervisor, Dave, is always hovering around asking if the data is ready. Dave is one of those guys who prints out his emails to read them. He literally has a stack of printed emails on his desk. I tried showing him how to set up folders in Outlook, but he said he doesn't trust the cloud. Which is ridiculous because our entire database is backed up on a server in Ohio anyway. 

But yeah, I don't want to disappear or anything like that. I just wish I could have a long, uninterrupted break from having to do things.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It's pretty much all the time. Most days, like I said, there's just this low, gray mood running in the background. I mean, I still go to the lab and talk to people, so it's not like I'm crying in bed all day. But I don't really feel light or excited about anything. 

Like I was saying about the baking. I used to make these incredibly intricate sourdough loaves. I even cultivated my own starter—I named him Dough-B-Wan Kenobi. Cultivating wild yeast is actually a fascinating biological process. You have to feed it equal parts flour and water by weight, not volume, because the density of different flours can vary wildly depending on the ambient humidity. During the summer, when the lab's HVAC system was acting up—which is a whole other story, because the facilities management team outsourced their maintenance to this third-party contractor who never actually shows up on time—my starter was fermenting way too fast. I had to start keeping it in the fridge just to slow down the metabolic rate of the yeast. And then when you bake it, you need a Dutch oven to trap the steam so the crust gets that perfect blistered texture... 

But yeah, I haven't fed Dough-B-Wan in months. He's probably dead in the back of the fridge. I just don't have the energy to care. It's just that same gray feeling, pretty much every day.

I don't really think so. It feels pretty much the same whether it's summer or winter. 

Speaking of winter, though, the draft in that bungalow is just unbelievable. The windows are those old single-pane glass ones with the original sash weights inside the frames. They look beautiful, but the thermal efficiency is practically zero. I actually bought this infrared thermometer gun—mostly just to check the calibration on the incubators at the lab, but I brought it home one weekend—and the temperature differential near the glass was almost fifteen degrees compared to the center of the living room. I tried putting up that plastic shrink-wrap stuff you use a hairdryer on to seal them, but the adhesive pulled the paint right off the wood trim when I took it down in the spring. The paint is probably lead-based, too, considering the house was built in the thirties, which means I really shouldn't be disturbing it without a proper P100 particulate respirator. 

Dave actually ordered the wrong respirators for the lab last month. He got just the basic N95s, which don't do anything for the volatile organic compounds we work with, and when I tried to explain the difference in filtration media to him, he just stared at me. 

But anyway, sorry. What was the question? Oh, right, the seasons. No, I haven't noticed any difference with the weather or the time of year. It's just always kind of flat.

I tried putting up that plastic shrink-wrap stuff you use a hairdryer on to seal them, but the adhesive pulled the paint right off the wood trim when I took it down in the spring. The paint is probably lead-based, too, considering the house was built in the thirties, which means I really shouldn't be disturbing it without a proper P100 particulate respirator. \n\nDave actually ordered the wrong respirators for the lab last month. He got just the basic N95s, which don't do anything for the volatile organic compounds we work with, and when I tried to explain the difference in filtration media to him, he just stared at me. \n\nBut anyway, sorry. What was the question? Oh, right, the seasons. No, I haven't noticed any difference with the weather or the time of year. It's just always kind of flat.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any activities that you still enjoy or look forward to?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. I mean, I'm still getting my lab work done, so I'm functioning, I guess. But even that is a struggle. Grant drafts and routine project decisions are just taking noticeably longer than they used to. 

I used to have all these hobbies that structured my downtime, but I've stopped keeping up with them. Like I mentioned, the sourdough baking is gone. And my bonsai trees... I had this beautiful little Juniperus procumbens that I was training in a cascade style. The wiring process is incredibly delicate because if you apply too much pressure, you can snap the cambium layer and the whole branch dies. You have to use anodized aluminum wire, which is softer than copper, and wrap it at exactly a 45-degree angle. I had this whole specialized tool kit I ordered from Japan—concave cutters, knob cutters, the works. I used to spend hours just pruning and wiring. But I haven't watered them in weeks, so they're probably all dead now, just like the sourdough starter. 

And my telescope... I had this whole plan to build a motorized equatorial mount for my Dobsonian reflector so I could do some astrophotography. I even bought the stepper motors and an Arduino board to program the tracking rates. The math for calculating the sidereal tracking rate is actually pretty straightforward, but you have to account for the gear reduction ratio of the stepper motor assembly, which means calculating the exact number of microsteps per second. I had the whole schematic drawn up on my whiteboard at work. Dave actually erased part of it by accident when he was trying to show me his new organizational chart, which made absolutely no sense because he grouped the analytical chemists with the materials science team. 

Anyway... um... what were we talking about? Oh, right. Enjoying things. No, I don't really look forward to anything anymore. I just go to work, come home, and sit there.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. My appetite is pretty much exactly the same as it's always been. I eat the same things, mostly. 

Actually, I've been eating a lot of those pre-packaged quinoa bowls for lunch lately. The ones you just microwave for sixty seconds. The packaging on those is interesting, though—they use a proprietary polymer blend that allows for steam venting without the need to puncture the film. It's a really elegant solution to the pressure differential problem you get when superheating moisture-rich foods in a sealed environment. I was reading a paper the other day about how they're trying to adapt that same polymer technology for sterile medical equipment transport, but the tensile strength degrades too rapidly under UV sterilization protocols. 

Which reminds me, the UV sterilizer in our cell culture hood at the lab is completely shot. The bulb has been flickering for a week, and I told Dave we need to replace it because the efficacy of UVC light at 254 nanometers drops off a cliff once the mercury vapor inside the tube starts to deplete. But he just put a sticky note on it that says "Still works mostly!" Which is just... I mean, you can't "mostly" sterilize an environment. It's a binary state. Either it's sterile or it's contaminated. I ended up having to spray down the entire hood with 70% ethanol, which takes forever to evaporate because the ambient humidity in the lab is so high right now. 

But, um, sorry. You asked about my appetite, right? Yeah, no changes there. I just eat because I have to.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. My sleep hasn't changed. I'm sleeping about the same as always. 

I did get a new mattress a few months ago, though. One of those memory foam ones that comes compressed in a box. The expansion process is actually fascinating—it relies on the polyurethane foam's cellular structure pulling in ambient air to return to its original shape after being vacuum-sealed. But the off-gassing when you first open it is intense. It's mostly volatile organic compounds, specifically toluene diisocyanate, which they use in the manufacturing process. I actually brought home one of the portable VOC monitors from the lab just to see how high the parts-per-million would get in my bedroom. It peaked at around 3 ppm the first night, which is technically below the OSHA permissible exposure limit, but I still kept the window open. 

Speaking of the VOC monitor, Dave managed to drop the primary one into a vat of distilled water last week. Which, ironically, is probably the only liquid in the lab that wouldn't immediately corrode the sensor array, but it still shorted out the main circuit board. I told him we needed to pack it in silica gel immediately to draw the moisture out, but he just tried to dry it with a paper towel. A paper towel! The capillary action of the cellulose fibers isn't nearly strong enough to pull moisture out from underneath the surface-mounted components. I had to requisition a replacement board, which took three hours of paperwork because our new purchasing system requires dual authorization for anything over two hundred dollars. 

But, um... wait. Sleep. Right. No, my sleep is fine. I haven't noticed any trouble with it.

It made this horrendous squealing noise. I actually tried taking the motor apart on the kitchen table—I work in a lab calibrating physical testing equipment, so I figured, how hard can a vacuum be, right? But everything inside is glued together now. You can't even repair your own appliances anymore without voiding some warranty or needing a specialized screwdriver. It took me two hours just to get the plastic casing off, and I ended up just leaving all the pieces spread out on the counter. \n\nBut anyway, um, what was I saying? Right, I'm just... I'm just really tired. I don't have the energy for much of anything lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I guess... well, most days there’s just this low, gray mood running in the background. I mean, I still go to the lab and talk to people just fine. We actually just got a new batch of force gauges in from a supplier in Germany, which is usually interesting because their calibration standards are slightly different, but... I don't know, I just don't feel light or excited about things anymore. \n\nLike baking. Or my telescope. I used to love checking the telescope on clear nights. Though honestly, lately the light pollution from that new car dealership down the highway is just awful. They put up these massive LED floodlights that stay on all night. It completely ruins the viewing for the Orion Nebula, which used to be my favorite thing to track. I actually called the city council about their zoning permits for commercial lighting, because there are dark-sky ordinances they're supposed to follow, you know? But they just transfer you to three different departments and then put you on hold forever, and the hold music is always that same compressed jazz loop. \n\nBut, um... wait, what was the question? Oh, right, enjoying things. Yeah, even if the sky is clear, I just don't really get that spark from the telescope or baking or much of anything right now. It's just that same gray feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to be alive or wishing you could just disappear?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say that. I don't want to die or anything. I'm just... exhausted. It's more like I just want to pause everything for a while. \n\nLike when my computer at work decides to run a mandatory system update right in the middle of a calibration sequence. You know how it is, the screen goes blue, and it says \"Do not turn off your computer,\" and it just sits there at 14% for an hour. It's so frustrating because we have these strict deadlines for the quarterly reports, and my supervisor, Dave, is always hovering around asking if the data is ready. Dave is one of those guys who prints out his emails to read them. He literally has a stack of printed emails on his desk. I tried showing him how to set up folders in Outlook, but he said he doesn't trust the cloud. Which is ridiculous because our entire database is backed up on a server in Ohio anyway. \n\nBut yeah, I don't want to disappear or anything like that. I just wish I could have a long, uninterrupted break from having to do things.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, this sort of low baseline has been going on since shortly after we moved into the older house, which was a little over two years ago. It’s a 1930s bungalow, which everyone said had such great character, but honestly, the plumbing is just a nightmare. The pipes are all original galvanized steel, which means they corrode from the inside out and restrict the water flow. I actually brought a water sample into the lab a few months ago to run it through the mass spectrometer just to check the iron and lead levels. Dave—my supervisor, the one with the printed emails—told me I wasn't supposed to use company equipment for personal diagnostics, but it literally took three minutes during my lunch break. And the water pressure is so bad anyway, you can barely rinse the shampoo out of your hair. I tried replacing the showerhead with one of those high-efficiency aerating ones, but the threading on the pipe was completely stripped by the previous owner, so I had to use half a roll of Teflon tape just to get a seal. And even then it drips. \n\nBut, um... sorry, I lost my train of thought. Right, the timeline. Yeah, it's been a little over two years now of just feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Is this low, gray mood you mentioned something that you feel all the time, or are there days when it lifts a bit?\n\nPatient: It's pretty much all the time. Most days, like I said, there's just this low, gray mood running in the background. I mean, I still go to the lab and talk to people, so it's not like I'm crying in bed all day. But I don't really feel light or excited about anything. \n\nLike I was saying about the baking. I used to make these incredibly intricate sourdough loaves. I even cultivated my own starter—I named him Dough-B-Wan Kenobi. Cultivating wild yeast is actually a fascinating biological process. You have to feed it equal parts flour and water by weight, not volume, because the density of different flours can vary wildly depending on the ambient humidity. During the summer, when the lab's HVAC system was acting up—which is a whole other story, because the facilities management team outsourced their maintenance to this third-party contractor who never actually shows up on time—my starter was fermenting way too fast. I had to start keeping it in the fridge just to slow down the metabolic rate of the yeast. And then when you bake it, you need a Dutch oven to trap the steam so the crust gets that perfect blistered texture... \n\nBut yeah, I haven't fed Dough-B-Wan in months. He's probably dead in the back of the fridge. I just don't have the energy to care. It's just that same gray feeling, pretty much every day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your low, gray mood changes at all with the seasons?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. It feels pretty much the same whether it's summer or winter. \n\nSpeaking of winter, though, the draft in that bungalow is just unbelievable. The windows are those old single-pane glass ones with the original sash weights inside the frames. They look beautiful, but the thermal efficiency is practically zero. I actually bought this infrared thermometer gun—mostly just to check the calibration on the incubators at the lab, but I brought it home one weekend—and the temperature differential near the glass was almost fifteen degrees compared to the center of the living room. I tried putting up that plastic shrink-wrap stuff you use a hairdryer on to seal them, but the adhesive pulled the paint right off the wood trim when I took it down in the spring. The paint is probably lead-based, too, considering the house was built in the thirties, which means I really shouldn't be disturbing it without a proper P100 particulate respirator. \n\nDave actually ordered the wrong respirators for the lab last month. He got just the basic N95s, which don't do anything for the volatile organic compounds we work with, and when I tried to explain the difference in filtration media to him, he just stared at me. \n\nBut anyway, sorry. What was the question? Oh, right, the seasons. No, I haven't noticed any difference with the weather or the time of year. It's just always kind of flat.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I'm still getting my lab work done, so I'm functioning, I guess. But even that is a struggle. Grant drafts and routine project decisions are just taking noticeably longer than they used to. \n\nI used to have all these hobbies that structured my downtime, but I've stopped keeping up with them. Like I mentioned, the sourdough baking is gone. And my bonsai trees... I had this beautiful little Juniperus procumbens that I was training in a cascade style. The wiring process is incredibly delicate because if you apply too much pressure, you can snap the cambium layer and the whole branch dies. You have to use anodized aluminum wire, which is softer than copper, and wrap it at exactly a 45-degree angle. I had this whole specialized tool kit I ordered from Japan—concave cutters, knob cutters, the works. I used to spend hours just pruning and wiring. But I haven't watered them in weeks, so they're probably all dead now, just like the sourdough starter. \n\nAnd my telescope... I had this whole plan to build a motorized equatorial mount for my Dobsonian reflector so I could do some astrophotography. I even bought the stepper motors and an Arduino board to program the tracking rates. The math for calculating the sidereal tracking rate is actually pretty straightforward, but you have to account for the gear reduction ratio of the stepper motor assembly, which means calculating the exact number of microsteps per second. I had the whole schematic drawn up on my whiteboard at work. Dave actually erased part of it by accident when he was trying to show me his new organizational chart, which made absolutely no sense because he grouped the analytical chemists with the materials science team. \n\nAnyway... um... what were we talking about? Oh, right. Enjoying things. No, I don't really look forward to anything anymore. I just go to work, come home, and sit there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite recently?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. My appetite is pretty much exactly the same as it's always been. I eat the same things, mostly. \n\nActually, I've been eating a lot of those pre-packaged quinoa bowls for lunch lately. The ones you just microwave for sixty seconds. The packaging on those is interesting, though—they use a proprietary polymer blend that allows for steam venting without the need to puncture the film. It's a really elegant solution to the pressure differential problem you get when superheating moisture-rich foods in a sealed environment. I was reading a paper the other day about how they're trying to adapt that same polymer technology for sterile medical equipment transport, but the tensile strength degrades too rapidly under UV sterilization protocols. \n\nWhich reminds me, the UV sterilizer in our cell culture hood at the lab is completely shot. The bulb has been flickering for a week, and I told Dave we need to replace it because the efficacy of UVC light at 254 nanometers drops off a cliff once the mercury vapor inside the tube starts to deplete. But he just put a sticky note on it that says \"Still works mostly!\" Which is just... I mean, you can't \"mostly\" sterilize an environment. It's a binary state. Either it's sterile or it's contaminated. I ended up having to spray down the entire hood with 70% ethanol, which takes forever to evaporate because the ambient humidity in the lab is so high right now. \n\nBut, um, sorry. You asked about my appetite, right? Yeah, no changes there. I just eat because I have to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. My sleep hasn't changed. I'm sleeping about the same as always. \n\nI did get a new mattress a few months ago, though. One of those memory foam ones that comes compressed in a box. The expansion process is actually fascinating—it relies on the polyurethane foam's cellular structure pulling in ambient air to return to its original shape after being vacuum-sealed. But the off-gassing when you first open it is intense. It's mostly volatile organic compounds, specifically toluene diisocyanate, which they use in the manufacturing process. I actually brought home one of the portable VOC monitors from the lab just to see how high the parts-per-million would get in my bedroom. It peaked at around 3 ppm the first night, which is technically below the OSHA permissible exposure limit, but I still kept the window open. \n\nSpeaking of the VOC monitor, Dave managed to drop the primary one into a vat of distilled water last week. Which, ironically, is probably the only liquid in the lab that wouldn't immediately corrode the sensor array, but it still shorted out the main circuit board. I told him we needed to pack it in silica gel immediately to draw the moisture out, but he just tried to dry it with a paper towel. A paper towel! The capillary action of the cellulose fibers isn't nearly strong enough to pull moisture out from underneath the surface-mounted components. I had to requisition a replacement board, which took three hours of paperwork because our new purchasing system requires dual authorization for anything over two hundred dollars. \n\nBut, um... wait. Sleep. Right. No, my sleep is fine. I haven't noticed any trouble with it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

My appetite is pretty much exactly the same as it's always been. I eat the same things, mostly. \n\nActually, I've been eating a lot of those pre-packaged quinoa bowls for lunch lately. The ones you just microwave for sixty seconds. The packaging on those is interesting, though—they use a proprietary polymer blend that allows for steam venting without the need to puncture the film. It's a really elegant solution to the pressure differential problem you get when superheating moisture-rich foods in a sealed environment. I was reading a paper the other day about how they're trying to adapt that same polymer technology for sterile medical equipment transport, but the tensile strength degrades too rapidly under UV sterilization protocols. \n\nWhich reminds me, the UV sterilizer in our cell culture hood at the lab is completely shot. The bulb has been flickering for a week, and I told Dave we need to replace it because the efficacy of UVC light at 254 nanometers drops off a cliff once the mercury vapor inside the tube starts to deplete. But he just put a sticky note on it that says \"Still works mostly!\" Which is just... I mean, you can't \"mostly\" sterilize an environment. It's a binary state. Either it's sterile or it's contaminated. I ended up having to spray down the entire hood with 70% ethanol, which takes forever to evaporate because the ambient humidity in the lab is so high right now. \n\nBut, um, sorry. You asked about my appetite, right? Yeah, no changes there. I just eat because I have to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. My sleep hasn't changed. I'm sleeping about the same as always. \n\nI did get a new mattress a few months ago, though. One of those memory foam ones that comes compressed in a box. The expansion process is actually fascinating—it relies on the polyurethane foam's cellular structure pulling in ambient air to return to its original shape after being vacuum-sealed. But the off-gassing when you first open it is intense. It's mostly volatile organic compounds, specifically toluene diisocyanate, which they use in the manufacturing process. I actually brought home one of the portable VOC monitors from the lab just to see how high the parts-per-million would get in my bedroom. It peaked at around 3 ppm the first night, which is technically below the OSHA permissible exposure limit, but I still kept the window open. \n\nSpeaking of the VOC monitor, Dave managed to drop the primary one into a vat of distilled water last week. Which, ironically, is probably the only liquid in the lab that wouldn't immediately corrode the sensor array, but it still shorted out the main circuit board. I told him we needed to pack it in silica gel immediately to draw the moisture out, but he just tried to dry it with a paper towel. A paper towel! The capillary action of the cellulose fibers isn't nearly strong enough to pull moisture out from underneath the surface-mounted components. I had to requisition a replacement board, which took three hours of paperwork because our new purchasing system requires dual authorization for anything over two hundred dollars. \n\nBut, um... wait. Sleep. Right. No, my sleep is fine. I haven't noticed any trouble with it.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any activities, no matter how small, that you still find slightly enjoyable or that bring you a tiny bit of pleasure?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really. I can't think of anything. I mean, even the stuff I used to force myself to do just feels like a massive chore now. 

I was trying to organize my desk at home the other day, just to have a clean surface, and I found this old collection of fountain pens I used to be obsessed with. I had this one, a Pilot Custom 823, which uses a vacuum filling mechanism instead of a standard piston or cartridge. It's brilliant engineering, really. You pull the plunger back, which creates negative pressure in the barrel, and when you push it down and it passes a wider section near the feed, the vacuum breaks and atmospheric pressure shoots the ink up into the pen. It holds an absurd amount of ink, like 2.5 milliliters, which is huge for a fountain pen. But the O-rings on the plunger rod need to be lubricated with pure silicone grease periodically, otherwise the friction degrades the rubber and ruins the seal. 

I actually brought a tiny tub of industrial-grade silicone grease from the lab specifically for that pen a few years ago. It's the same grease we use to seal the joints on the rotary evaporator when we're pulling a high vacuum. Although, last week I caught Dave trying to use petroleum jelly on the rotovap joints. Petroleum jelly! On standard taper glass joints! I had to explain to him that petroleum-based lubricants will literally dissolve the rubber gaskets in the vacuum pump if the vapors get pulled through the system. Not to mention it's completely inadequate for holding anything below 100 millibar. He just stared at me like I was speaking Aramaic. I swear, sometimes I wonder how he even passed general chemistry. 

But, um... sorry. What was the question? Oh, right. Enjoying small things. No. No, I don't really find pleasure in anything right now. Even the pens just went back in the drawer.

It took me two hours just to get the plastic casing off, and I ended up just leaving all the pieces spread out on the counter. \n\nBut anyway, um, what was I saying? Right, I'm just... I'm just really tired. I don't have the energy for much of anything lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I guess... well, most days there’s just this low, gray mood running in the background. I mean, I still go to the lab and talk to people just fine. We actually just got a new batch of force gauges in from a supplier in Germany, which is usually interesting because their calibration standards are slightly different, but... I don't know, I just don't feel light or excited about things anymore. \n\nLike baking. Or my telescope. I used to love checking the telescope on clear nights. Though honestly, lately the light pollution from that new car dealership down the highway is just awful. They put up these massive LED floodlights that stay on all night. It completely ruins the viewing for the Orion Nebula, which used to be my favorite thing to track. I actually called the city council about their zoning permits for commercial lighting, because there are dark-sky ordinances they're supposed to follow, you know? But they just transfer you to three different departments and then put you on hold forever, and the hold music is always that same compressed jazz loop. \n\nBut, um... wait, what was the question? Oh, right, enjoying things. Yeah, even if the sky is clear, I just don't really get that spark from the telescope or baking or much of anything right now. It's just that same gray feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to be alive or wishing you could just disappear?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say that. I don't want to die or anything. I'm just... exhausted. It's more like I just want to pause everything for a while. \n\nLike when my computer at work decides to run a mandatory system update right in the middle of a calibration sequence. You know how it is, the screen goes blue, and it says \"Do not turn off your computer,\" and it just sits there at 14% for an hour. It's so frustrating because we have these strict deadlines for the quarterly reports, and my supervisor, Dave, is always hovering around asking if the data is ready. Dave is one of those guys who prints out his emails to read them. He literally has a stack of printed emails on his desk. I tried showing him how to set up folders in Outlook, but he said he doesn't trust the cloud. Which is ridiculous because our entire database is backed up on a server in Ohio anyway. \n\nBut yeah, I don't want to disappear or anything like that. I just wish I could have a long, uninterrupted break from having to do things.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, this sort of low baseline has been going on since shortly after we moved into the older house, which was a little over two years ago. It’s a 1930s bungalow, which everyone said had such great character, but honestly, the plumbing is just a nightmare. The pipes are all original galvanized steel, which means they corrode from the inside out and restrict the water flow. I actually brought a water sample into the lab a few months ago to run it through the mass spectrometer just to check the iron and lead levels. Dave—my supervisor, the one with the printed emails—told me I wasn't supposed to use company equipment for personal diagnostics, but it literally took three minutes during my lunch break. And the water pressure is so bad anyway, you can barely rinse the shampoo out of your hair. I tried replacing the showerhead with one of those high-efficiency aerating ones, but the threading on the pipe was completely stripped by the previous owner, so I had to use half a roll of Teflon tape just to get a seal. And even then it drips. \n\nBut, um... sorry, I lost my train of thought. Right, the timeline. Yeah, it's been a little over two years now of just feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Is this low, gray mood you mentioned something that you feel all the time, or are there days when it lifts a bit?\n\nPatient: It's pretty much all the time. Most days, like I said, there's just this low, gray mood running in the background. I mean, I still go to the lab and talk to people, so it's not like I'm crying in bed all day. But I don't really feel light or excited about anything. \n\nLike I was saying about the baking. I used to make these incredibly intricate sourdough loaves. I even cultivated my own starter—I named him Dough-B-Wan Kenobi. Cultivating wild yeast is actually a fascinating biological process. You have to feed it equal parts flour and water by weight, not volume, because the density of different flours can vary wildly depending on the ambient humidity. During the summer, when the lab's HVAC system was acting up—which is a whole other story, because the facilities management team outsourced their maintenance to this third-party contractor who never actually shows up on time—my starter was fermenting way too fast. I had to start keeping it in the fridge just to slow down the metabolic rate of the yeast. And then when you bake it, you need a Dutch oven to trap the steam so the crust gets that perfect blistered texture... \n\nBut yeah, I haven't fed Dough-B-Wan in months. He's probably dead in the back of the fridge. I just don't have the energy to care. It's just that same gray feeling, pretty much every day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your low, gray mood changes at all with the seasons?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. It feels pretty much the same whether it's summer or winter. \n\nSpeaking of winter, though, the draft in that bungalow is just unbelievable. The windows are those old single-pane glass ones with the original sash weights inside the frames. They look beautiful, but the thermal efficiency is practically zero. I actually bought this infrared thermometer gun—mostly just to check the calibration on the incubators at the lab, but I brought it home one weekend—and the temperature differential near the glass was almost fifteen degrees compared to the center of the living room. I tried putting up that plastic shrink-wrap stuff you use a hairdryer on to seal them, but the adhesive pulled the paint right off the wood trim when I took it down in the spring. The paint is probably lead-based, too, considering the house was built in the thirties, which means I really shouldn't be disturbing it without a proper P100 particulate respirator. \n\nDave actually ordered the wrong respirators for the lab last month. He got just the basic N95s, which don't do anything for the volatile organic compounds we work with, and when I tried to explain the difference in filtration media to him, he just stared at me. \n\nBut anyway, sorry. What was the question? Oh, right, the seasons. No, I haven't noticed any difference with the weather or the time of year. It's just always kind of flat.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I'm still getting my lab work done, so I'm functioning, I guess. But even that is a struggle. Grant drafts and routine project decisions are just taking noticeably longer than they used to. \n\nI used to have all these hobbies that structured my downtime, but I've stopped keeping up with them. Like I mentioned, the sourdough baking is gone. And my bonsai trees... I had this beautiful little Juniperus procumbens that I was training in a cascade style. The wiring process is incredibly delicate because if you apply too much pressure, you can snap the cambium layer and the whole branch dies. You have to use anodized aluminum wire, which is softer than copper, and wrap it at exactly a 45-degree angle. I had this whole specialized tool kit I ordered from Japan—concave cutters, knob cutters, the works. I used to spend hours just pruning and wiring. But I haven't watered them in weeks, so they're probably all dead now, just like the sourdough starter. \n\nAnd my telescope... I had this whole plan to build a motorized equatorial mount for my Dobsonian reflector so I could do some astrophotography. I even bought the stepper motors and an Arduino board to program the tracking rates. The math for calculating the sidereal tracking rate is actually pretty straightforward, but you have to account for the gear reduction ratio of the stepper motor assembly, which means calculating the exact number of microsteps per second. I had the whole schematic drawn up on my whiteboard at work. Dave actually erased part of it by accident when he was trying to show me his new organizational chart, which made absolutely no sense because he grouped the analytical chemists with the materials science team. \n\nAnyway... um... what were we talking about? Oh, right. Enjoying things. No, I don't really look forward to anything anymore. I just go to work, come home, and sit there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite recently?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. My appetite is pretty much exactly the same as it's always been. I eat the same things, mostly. \n\nActually, I've been eating a lot of those pre-packaged quinoa bowls for lunch lately. The ones you just microwave for sixty seconds. The packaging on those is interesting, though—they use a proprietary polymer blend that allows for steam venting without the need to puncture the film. It's a really elegant solution to the pressure differential problem you get when superheating moisture-rich foods in a sealed environment. I was reading a paper the other day about how they're trying to adapt that same polymer technology for sterile medical equipment transport, but the tensile strength degrades too rapidly under UV sterilization protocols. \n\nWhich reminds me, the UV sterilizer in our cell culture hood at the lab is completely shot. The bulb has been flickering for a week, and I told Dave we need to replace it because the efficacy of UVC light at 254 nanometers drops off a cliff once the mercury vapor inside the tube starts to deplete. But he just put a sticky note on it that says \"Still works mostly!\" Which is just... I mean, you can't \"mostly\" sterilize an environment. It's a binary state. Either it's sterile or it's contaminated. I ended up having to spray down the entire hood with 70% ethanol, which takes forever to evaporate because the ambient humidity in the lab is so high right now. \n\nBut, um, sorry. You asked about my appetite, right? Yeah, no changes there. I just eat because I have to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. My sleep hasn't changed. I'm sleeping about the same as always. \n\nI did get a new mattress a few months ago, though. One of those memory foam ones that comes compressed in a box. The expansion process is actually fascinating—it relies on the polyurethane foam's cellular structure pulling in ambient air to return to its original shape after being vacuum-sealed. But the off-gassing when you first open it is intense. It's mostly volatile organic compounds, specifically toluene diisocyanate, which they use in the manufacturing process. I actually brought home one of the portable VOC monitors from the lab just to see how high the parts-per-million would get in my bedroom. It peaked at around 3 ppm the first night, which is technically below the OSHA permissible exposure limit, but I still kept the window open. \n\nSpeaking of the VOC monitor, Dave managed to drop the primary one into a vat of distilled water last week. Which, ironically, is probably the only liquid in the lab that wouldn't immediately corrode the sensor array, but it still shorted out the main circuit board. I told him we needed to pack it in silica gel immediately to draw the moisture out, but he just tried to dry it with a paper towel. A paper towel! The capillary action of the cellulose fibers isn't nearly strong enough to pull moisture out from underneath the surface-mounted components. I had to requisition a replacement board, which took three hours of paperwork because our new purchasing system requires dual authorization for anything over two hundred dollars. \n\nBut, um... wait. Sleep. Right. No, my sleep is fine. I haven't noticed any trouble with it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities, no matter how small, that you still find slightly enjoyable or that bring you a tiny bit of pleasure?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I can't think of anything. I mean, even the stuff I used to force myself to do just feels like a massive chore now. \n\nI was trying to organize my desk at home the other day, just to have a clean surface, and I found this old collection of fountain pens I used to be obsessed with. I had this one, a Pilot Custom 823, which uses a vacuum filling mechanism instead of a standard piston or cartridge. It's brilliant engineering, really. You pull the plunger back, which creates negative pressure in the barrel, and when you push it down and it passes a wider section near the feed, the vacuum breaks and atmospheric pressure shoots the ink up into the pen. It holds an absurd amount of ink, like 2.5 milliliters, which is huge for a fountain pen. But the O-rings on the plunger rod need to be lubricated with pure silicone grease periodically, otherwise the friction degrades the rubber and ruins the seal. \n\nI actually brought a tiny tub of industrial-grade silicone grease from the lab specifically for that pen a few years ago. It's the same grease we use to seal the joints on the rotary evaporator when we're pulling a high vacuum. Although, last week I caught Dave trying to use petroleum jelly on the rotovap joints. Petroleum jelly! On standard taper glass joints! I had to explain to him that petroleum-based lubricants will literally dissolve the rubber gaskets in the vacuum pump if the vapors get pulled through the system. Not to mention it's completely inadequate for holding anything below 100 millibar. He just stared at me like I was speaking Aramaic. I swear, sometimes I wonder how he even passed general chemistry. \n\nBut, um... sorry. What was the question? Oh, right. Enjoying small things. No. No, I don't really find pleasure in anything right now. Even the pens just went back in the drawer.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "content": "Return the JSON now."

No, I wouldn't say that. I don't feel worthless or like a burden, really. I just... I'm just here, you know? Doing my job. 

Speaking of my job, I was actually looking at the structural integrity of the shelving units in the storage room the other day. They're these standard heavy-duty steel wire racks, but the way they're assembled relies on these plastic slip sleeves that wedge between the shelf collar and the vertical post. It's a friction fit, so the heavier the load, the tighter the grip. It's a clever design, assuming the weight distribution is relatively even. But someone—and I'm not going to name names, but his initials are D.A.V.E.—decided to stack four cases of concentrated sulfuric acid on the very edge of the top shelf. The sheer stress on that single plastic sleeve must have been pushing its structural limits. Polypropylene has decent compressive strength, but under sustained, asymmetrical load, it will eventually experience creep and deform. 

I actually had to spend two hours reorganizing the entire rack so the center of mass was lower and properly distributed. And while I was doing that, I noticed the emergency eyewash station next to the shelves hadn't been flushed in over a month. The protocol clearly states it needs to be run weekly to prevent microbial growth in the stagnant water line. Acanthamoeba can thrive in those conditions, and if you get that in your eye while trying to wash out a chemical splash, you're trading a burn for a potentially blinding infection. I ended up flushing it myself and logging it, but the water that came out first was this distinct rusty orange color. The iron oxidation in the pipes is getting worse, which means the facility management hasn't been maintaining the anti-corrosion inhibitors in the building's water supply. 

But, um... wait, what were we talking about? Worthless? Oh. No, no. I don't feel like a burden to anyone. I just kind of keep to myself.

During the summer, when the lab's HVAC system was acting up—which is a whole other story, because the facilities management team outsourced their maintenance to this third-party contractor who never actually shows up on time—my starter was fermenting way too fast. I had to start keeping it in the fridge just to slow down the metabolic rate of the yeast. And then when you bake it, you need a Dutch oven to trap the steam so the crust gets that perfect blistered texture... \n\nBut yeah, I haven't fed Dough-B-Wan in months. He's probably dead in the back of the fridge. I just don't have the energy to care. It's just that same gray feeling, pretty much every day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your low, gray mood changes at all with the seasons?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. It feels pretty much the same whether it's summer or winter. \n\nSpeaking of winter, though, the draft in that bungalow is just unbelievable. The windows are those old single-pane glass ones with the original sash weights inside the frames. They look beautiful, but the thermal efficiency is practically zero. I actually bought this infrared thermometer gun—mostly just to check the calibration on the incubators at the lab, but I brought it home one weekend—and the temperature differential near the glass was almost fifteen degrees compared to the center of the living room. I tried putting up that plastic shrink-wrap stuff you use a hairdryer on to seal them, but the adhesive pulled the paint right off the wood trim when I took it down in the spring. Grant drafts and routine project decisions are just taking noticeably longer than they used to. \n\nI used to have all these hobbies that structured my downtime, but I've stopped keeping up with them. Like I mentioned, the sourdough baking is gone. And my bonsai trees... I had this beautiful little Juniperus procumbens that I was training in a cascade style. The wiring process is incredibly delicate because if you apply too much pressure, you can snap the cambium layer and the whole branch dies. You have to use anodized aluminum wire, which is softer than copper, and wrap it at exactly a 45-degree angle. I had this whole specialized tool kit I ordered from Japan—concave cutters, knob cutters, the works. I used to spend hours just pruning and wiring. But I haven't watered them in weeks, so they're probably all dead now, just like the sourdough starter. \n\nAnd my telescope... I had this whole plan to build a motorized equatorial mount for my Dobsonian reflector so I could do some astrophotography. I even bought the stepper motors and an Arduino board to program the tracking rates. The math for calculating the sidereal tracking rate is actually pretty straightforward, but you have to account for the gear reduction ratio of the stepper motor assembly, which means calculating the exact number of microsteps per second. I had the whole schematic drawn up on my whiteboard at work. Dave actually erased part of it by accident when he was trying to show me his new organizational chart, which made absolutely no sense because he grouped the analytical chemists with the materials science team. \n\nAnyway... um... what were we talking about? The ones you just microwave for sixty seconds. The packaging on those is interesting, though—they use a proprietary polymer blend that allows for steam venting without the need to puncture the film. It's a really elegant solution to the pressure differential problem you get when superheating moisture-rich foods in a sealed environment. I was reading a paper the other day about how they're trying to adapt that same polymer technology for sterile medical equipment transport, but the tensile strength degrades too rapidly under UV sterilization protocols. \n\nWhich reminds me, the UV sterilizer in our cell culture hood at the lab is completely shot. The bulb has been flickering for a week, and I told Dave we need to replace it because the efficacy of UVC light at 254 nanometers drops off a cliff once the mercury vapor inside the tube starts to deplete. I'm sleeping about the same as always. \n\nI did get a new mattress a few months ago, though. One of those memory foam ones that comes compressed in a box. But the off-gassing when you first open it is intense. It's mostly volatile organic compounds, specifically toluene diisocyanate, which they use in the manufacturing process. I actually brought home one of the portable VOC monitors from the lab just to see how high the parts-per-million would get in my bedroom. The capillary action of the cellulose fibers isn't nearly strong enough to pull moisture out from underneath the surface-mounted components. Sleep. Right. No, my sleep is fine. I haven't noticed any trouble with it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities, no matter how small, that you still find slightly enjoyable or that bring you a tiny bit of pleasure?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I can't think of anything. I mean, even the stuff I used to force myself to do just feels like a massive chore now. \n\nI was trying to organize my desk at home the other day, just to have a clean surface, and I found this old collection of fountain pens I used to be obsessed with. I had this one, a Pilot Custom 823, which uses a vacuum filling mechanism instead of a standard piston or cartridge. It's brilliant engineering, really. You pull the plunger back, which creates negative pressure in the barrel, and when you push it down and it passes a wider section near the feed, the vacuum breaks and atmospheric pressure shoots the ink up into the pen. It holds an absurd amount of ink, like 2.5 milliliters, which is huge for a fountain pen. But the O-rings on the plunger rod need to be lubricated with pure silicone grease periodically, otherwise the friction degrades the rubber and ruins the seal. \n\nI actually brought a tiny tub of industrial-grade silicone grease from the lab specifically for that pen a few years ago. Petroleum jelly! On standard taper glass joints! I had to explain to him that petroleum-based lubricants will literally dissolve the rubber gaskets in the vacuum pump if the vapors get pulled through the system. Not to mention it's completely inadequate for holding anything below 100 millibar. He just stared at me like I was speaking Aramaic. I swear, sometimes I wonder how he even passed general chemistry. \n\nBut, um... sorry. What was the question? Oh, right. Enjoying small things. No. No, I don't really find pleasure in anything right now. Even the pens just went back in the drawer.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or like you're a burden to others?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. Which, ironically, is probably the only liquid in the lab that wouldn't immediately corrode the sensor array, but it still shorted out the main circuit board. I told him we needed to pack it in silica gel immediately to draw the moisture out, but he just tried to dry it with a paper towel. A paper towel! The capillary action of the cellulose fibers isn't nearly strong enough to pull moisture out from underneath the surface-mounted components. I had to requisition a replacement board, which took three hours of paperwork because our new purchasing system requires dual authorization for anything over two hundred dollars. \n\nBut, um... wait. Sleep. Right. No, my sleep is fine. I haven't noticed any trouble with it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities, no matter how small, that you still find slightly enjoyable or that bring you a tiny bit of pleasure?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I can't think of anything. I mean, even the stuff I used to force myself to do just feels like a massive chore now. \n\nI was trying to organize my desk at home the other day, just to have a clean surface, and I found this old collection of fountain pens I used to be obsessed with. I had this one, a Pilot Custom 823, which uses a vacuum filling mechanism instead of a standard piston or cartridge. It's brilliant engineering, really. You pull the plunger back, which creates negative pressure in the barrel, and when you push it down and it passes a wider section near the feed, the vacuum breaks and atmospheric pressure shoots the ink up into the pen. It holds an absurd amount of ink, like 2.5 milliliters, which is huge for a fountain pen. But the O-rings on the plunger rod need to be lubricated with pure silicone grease periodically, otherwise the friction degrades the rubber and ruins the seal. \n\nI actually brought a tiny tub of industrial-grade silicone grease from the lab specifically for that pen a few years ago. It's the same grease we use to seal the joints on the rotary evaporator when we're pulling a high vacuum. Although, last week I caught Dave trying to use petroleum jelly on the rotovap joints. Petroleum jelly! On standard taper glass joints! I had to explain to him that petroleum-based lubricants will literally dissolve the rubber gaskets in the vacuum pump if the vapors get pulled through the system. Not to mention it's completely inadequate for holding anything below 100 millibar. He just stared at me like I was speaking Aramaic. I swear, sometimes I wonder how he even passed general chemistry. \n\nBut, um... sorry. What was the question? Oh, right. Enjoying small things. No. No, I don't really find pleasure in anything right now. Even the pens just went back in the drawer.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or like you're a burden to others?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I don't feel worthless or like a burden, really. I just... I'm just here, you know? Doing my job. \n\nSpeaking of my job, I was actually looking at the structural integrity of the shelving units in the storage room the other day. They're these standard heavy-duty steel wire racks, but the way they're assembled relies on these plastic slip sleeves that wedge between the shelf collar and the vertical post. It's a friction fit, so the heavier the load, the tighter the grip. It's a clever design, assuming the weight distribution is relatively even. But someone—and I'm not going to name names, but his initials are D.A.V.E.—decided to stack four cases of concentrated sulfuric acid on the very edge of the top shelf. The sheer stress on that single plastic sleeve must have been pushing its structural limits. Polypropylene has decent compressive strength, but under sustained, asymmetrical load, it will eventually experience creep and deform. \n\nI actually had to spend two hours reorganizing the entire rack so the center of mass was lower and properly distributed. And while I was doing that, I noticed the emergency eyewash station next to the shelves hadn't been flushed in over a month. The protocol clearly states it needs to be run weekly to prevent microbial growth in the stagnant water line. Acanthamoeba can thrive in those conditions, and if you get that in your eye while trying to wash out a chemical splash, you're trading a burn for a potentially blinding infection. I ended up flushing it myself and logging it, but the water that came out first was this distinct rusty orange color. The iron oxidation in the pipes is getting worse, which means the facility management hasn't been maintaining the anti-corrosion inhibitors in the building's water supply. \n\nBut, um... wait, what were we talking about? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, the time of year really doesn't make a difference. Most days there's just this... this low, gray mood running in the background. Like, I can still go to the lab and do my job and talk to people, but I don't really feel light or excited about things anymore. Even stuff I used to love, like baking or checking the telescope, it's just... dull. 

Speaking of the telescope, I actually had to recalibrate the primary mirror on my Dobsonian reflector last weekend. It's an 8-inch aperture, which is decent for amateur planetary observation, but the collimation was completely knocked out of alignment. I suspect it happened when the temperature dropped suddenly on Tuesday. The thermal expansion coefficient of the borosilicate glass mirror is pretty low, but the steel tube housing contracts much faster in the cold, which can warp the tension on the mounting springs. It's a delicate system. You have to use a laser collimator to align the secondary mirror to the center of the primary, and then adjust the primary so the laser reflects perfectly back onto itself. 

I was trying to explain the geometry of optical alignment to Dave when he asked what I did over the weekend. I told him about the parabolic curve of the primary mirror and how it focuses incoming light to a single point, and he asked if he could use it to melt ants like a magnifying glass. A magnifying glass! First of all, a telescope uses a concave mirror, not a convex lens, so the focal point is inside the tube. Secondly, the sheer disrespect for precision optical equipment... I mean, the aluminum coating on that mirror is only a few molecules thick. If you even breathe on it wrong, you can degrade the reflectivity. I had to walk away before I explained the physics of thermal energy concentration to him in a way that might have involved his own hand. 

But, um... sorry. What were you asking? Seasons? Right. No, winter or summer, it doesn't matter. It's just always that same gray feeling in the background.

I'm just really tired. Though honestly, lately the light pollution from that new car dealership down the highway is just awful. They put up these massive LED floodlights that stay on all night. It's just that same gray feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to be alive or wishing you could just disappear?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say that. I don't want to die or anything. I'm just... exhausted. It's more like I just want to pause everything for a while. \n\nLike when my computer at work decides to run a mandatory system update right in the middle of a calibration sequence. You know how it is, the screen goes blue, and it says \"Do not turn off your computer,\" and it just sits there at 14% for an hour. It's so frustrating because we have these strict deadlines for the quarterly reports, and my supervisor, Dave, is always hovering around asking if the data is ready. Dave is one of those guys who prints out his emails to read them. He literally has a stack of printed emails on his desk. I tried showing him how to set up folders in Outlook, but he said he doesn't trust the cloud. Which is ridiculous because our entire database is backed up on a server in Ohio anyway. \n\nBut yeah, I don't want to disappear or anything like that. I just wish I could have a long, uninterrupted break from having to do things.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, this sort of low baseline has been going on since shortly after we moved into the older house, which was a little over two years ago. It’s a 1930s bungalow, which everyone said had such great character, but honestly, the plumbing is just a nightmare. The pipes are all original galvanized steel, which means they corrode from the inside out and restrict the water flow. I actually brought a water sample into the lab a few months ago to run it through the mass spectrometer just to check the iron and lead levels. Dave—my supervisor, the one with the printed emails—told me I wasn't supposed to use company equipment for personal diagnostics, but it literally took three minutes during my lunch break. And the water pressure is so bad anyway, you can barely rinse the shampoo out of your hair. I tried replacing the showerhead with one of those high-efficiency aerating ones, but the threading on the pipe was completely stripped by the previous owner, so I had to use half a roll of Teflon tape just to get a seal. And even then it drips. \n\nBut, um... sorry, I lost my train of thought. Right, the timeline. Yeah, it's been a little over two years now of just feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Is this low, gray mood you mentioned something that you feel all the time, or are there days when it lifts a bit?\n\nPatient: It's pretty much all the time. Most days, like I said, there's just this low, gray mood running in the background. I mean, I still go to the lab and talk to people, so it's not like I'm crying in bed all day. But I don't really feel light or excited about anything. \n\nLike I was saying about the baking. I used to make these incredibly intricate sourdough loaves. I even cultivated my own starter—I named him Dough-B-Wan Kenobi. Cultivating wild yeast is actually a fascinating biological process. You have to feed it equal parts flour and water by weight, not volume, because the density of different flours can vary wildly depending on the ambient humidity. During the summer, when the lab's HVAC system was acting up—which is a whole other story, because the facilities management team outsourced their maintenance to this third-party contractor who never actually shows up on time—my starter was fermenting way too fast. I had to start keeping it in the fridge just to slow down the metabolic rate of the yeast. And then when you bake it, you need a Dutch oven to trap the steam so the crust gets that perfect blistered texture... \n\nBut yeah, I haven't fed Dough-B-Wan in months. He's probably dead in the back of the fridge. I just don't have the energy to care. It's just that same gray feeling, pretty much every day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your low, gray mood changes at all with the seasons?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. It feels pretty much the same whether it's summer or winter. \n\nSpeaking of winter, though, the draft in that bungalow is just unbelievable. The windows are those old single-pane glass ones with the original sash weights inside the frames. They look beautiful, but the thermal efficiency is practically zero. I actually bought this infrared thermometer gun—mostly just to check the calibration on the incubators at the lab, but I brought it home one weekend—and the temperature differential near the glass was almost fifteen degrees compared to the center of the living room. I tried putting up that plastic shrink-wrap stuff you use a hairdryer on to seal them, but the adhesive pulled the paint right off the wood trim when I took it down in the spring. The paint is probably lead-based, too, considering the house was built in the thirties, which means I really shouldn't be disturbing it without a proper P100 particulate respirator. \n\nDave actually ordered the wrong respirators for the lab last month. He got just the basic N95s, which don't do anything for the volatile organic compounds we work with, and when I tried to explain the difference in filtration media to him, he just stared at me. \n\nBut anyway, sorry. What was the question? Oh, right, the seasons. No, I haven't noticed any difference with the weather or the time of year. It's just always kind of flat.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I'm still getting my lab work done, so I'm functioning, I guess. But even that is a struggle. Grant drafts and routine project decisions are just taking noticeably longer than they used to. \n\nI used to have all these hobbies that structured my downtime, but I've stopped keeping up with them. Like I mentioned, the sourdough baking is gone. And my bonsai trees... I had this beautiful little Juniperus procumbens that I was training in a cascade style. The wiring process is incredibly delicate because if you apply too much pressure, you can snap the cambium layer and the whole branch dies. You have to use anodized aluminum wire, which is softer than copper, and wrap it at exactly a 45-degree angle. I had this whole specialized tool kit I ordered from Japan—concave cutters, knob cutters, the works. I used to spend hours just pruning and wiring. But I haven't watered them in weeks, so they're probably all dead now, just like the sourdough starter. \n\nAnd my telescope... I had this whole plan to build a motorized equatorial mount for my Dobsonian reflector so I could do some astrophotography. I even bought the stepper motors and an Arduino board to program the tracking rates. The math for calculating the sidereal tracking rate is actually pretty straightforward, but you have to account for the gear reduction ratio of the stepper motor assembly, which means calculating the exact number of microsteps per second. I had the whole schematic drawn up on my whiteboard at work. Dave actually erased part of it by accident when he was trying to show me his new organizational chart, which made absolutely no sense because he grouped the analytical chemists with the materials science team. \n\nAnyway... um... what were we talking about? Oh, right. Enjoying things. No, I don't really look forward to anything anymore. I just go to work, come home, and sit there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite recently?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. My appetite is pretty much exactly the same as it's always been. I eat the same things, mostly. \n\nActually, I've been eating a lot of those pre-packaged quinoa bowls for lunch lately. The ones you just microwave for sixty seconds. The packaging on those is interesting, though—they use a proprietary polymer blend that allows for steam venting without the need to puncture the film. It's a really elegant solution to the pressure differential problem you get when superheating moisture-rich foods in a sealed environment. I was reading a paper the other day about how they're trying to adapt that same polymer technology for sterile medical equipment transport, but the tensile strength degrades too rapidly under UV sterilization protocols. \n\nWhich reminds me, the UV sterilizer in our cell culture hood at the lab is completely shot. The bulb has been flickering for a week, and I told Dave we need to replace it because the efficacy of UVC light at 254 nanometers drops off a cliff once the mercury vapor inside the tube starts to deplete. But he just put a sticky note on it that says \"Still works mostly!\" Which is just... I mean, you can't \"mostly\" sterilize an environment. It's a binary state. Either it's sterile or it's contaminated. I ended up having to spray down the entire hood with 70% ethanol, which takes forever to evaporate because the ambient humidity in the lab is so high right now. \n\nBut, um, sorry. You asked about my appetite, right? Yeah, no changes there. I just eat because I have to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. My sleep hasn't changed. I'm sleeping about the same as always. \n\nI did get a new mattress a few months ago, though. One of those memory foam ones that comes compressed in a box. The expansion process is actually fascinating—it relies on the polyurethane foam's cellular structure pulling in ambient air to return to its original shape after being vacuum-sealed. But the off-gassing when you first open it is intense. It's mostly volatile organic compounds, specifically toluene diisocyanate, which they use in the manufacturing process. I actually brought home one of the portable VOC monitors from the lab just to see how high the parts-per-million would get in my bedroom. It peaked at around 3 ppm the first night, which is technically below the OSHA permissible exposure limit, but I still kept the window open. \n\nSpeaking of the VOC monitor, Dave managed to drop the primary one into a vat of distilled water last week. Which, ironically, is probably the only liquid in the lab that wouldn't immediately corrode the sensor array, but it still shorted out the main circuit board. I told him we needed to pack it in silica gel immediately to draw the moisture out, but he just tried to dry it with a paper towel. A paper towel! The capillary action of the cellulose fibers isn't nearly strong enough to pull moisture out from underneath the surface-mounted components. I had to requisition a replacement board, which took three hours of paperwork because our new purchasing system requires dual authorization for anything over two hundred dollars. \n\nBut, um... wait. Sleep. Right. No, my sleep is fine. I haven't noticed any trouble with it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities, no matter how small, that you still find slightly enjoyable or that bring you a tiny bit of pleasure?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I can't think of anything. I mean, even the stuff I used to force myself to do just feels like a massive chore now. \n\nI was trying to organize my desk at home the other day, just to have a clean surface, and I found this old collection of fountain pens I used to be obsessed with. I had this one, a Pilot Custom 823, which uses a vacuum filling mechanism instead of a standard piston or cartridge. It's brilliant engineering, really. You pull the plunger back, which creates negative pressure in the barrel, and when you push it down and it passes a wider section near the feed, the vacuum breaks and atmospheric pressure shoots the ink up into the pen. It holds an absurd amount of ink, like 2.5 milliliters, which is huge for a fountain pen. But the O-rings on the plunger rod need to be lubricated with pure silicone grease periodically, otherwise the friction degrades the rubber and ruins the seal. \n\nI actually brought a tiny tub of industrial-grade silicone grease from the lab specifically for that pen a few years ago. It's the same grease we use to seal the joints on the rotary evaporator when we're pulling a high vacuum. Although, last week I caught Dave trying to use petroleum jelly on the rotovap joints. Petroleum jelly! On standard taper glass joints! I had to explain to him that petroleum-based lubricants will literally dissolve the rubber gaskets in the vacuum pump if the vapors get pulled through the system. Not to mention it's completely inadequate for holding anything below 100 millibar. He just stared at me like I was speaking Aramaic. I swear, sometimes I wonder how he even passed general chemistry. \n\nBut, um... sorry. What was the question? Oh, right. Enjoying small things. No. No, I don't really find pleasure in anything right now. Even the pens just went back in the drawer.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or like you're a burden to others?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I don't feel worthless or like a burden, really. I just... I'm just here, you know? Doing my job. \n\nSpeaking of my job, I was actually looking at the structural integrity of the shelving units in the storage room the other day. They're these standard heavy-duty steel wire racks, but the way they're assembled relies on these plastic slip sleeves that wedge between the shelf collar and the vertical post. It's a friction fit, so the heavier the load, the tighter the grip. It's a clever design, assuming the weight distribution is relatively even. But someone—and I'm not going to name names, but his initials are D.A.V.E.—decided to stack four cases of concentrated sulfuric acid on the very edge of the top shelf. The sheer stress on that single plastic sleeve must have been pushing its structural limits. Polypropylene has decent compressive strength, but under sustained, asymmetrical load, it will eventually experience creep and deform. \n\nI actually had to spend two hours reorganizing the entire rack so the center of mass was lower and properly distributed. And while I was doing that, I noticed the emergency eyewash station next to the shelves hadn't been flushed in over a month. The protocol clearly states it needs to be run weekly to prevent microbial growth in the stagnant water line. Acanthamoeba can thrive in those conditions, and if you get that in your eye while trying to wash out a chemical splash, you're trading a burn for a potentially blinding infection. I ended up flushing it myself and logging it, but the water that came out first was this distinct rusty orange color. The iron oxidation in the pipes is getting worse, which means the facility management hasn't been maintaining the anti-corrosion inhibitors in the building's water supply. \n\nBut, um... wait, what were we talking about? Worthless? Oh. No, no. I don't feel like a burden to anyone. I just kind of keep to myself.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned earlier that your low, gray mood doesn't seem to change with the seasons, but can you think of any times during the year when you might feel slightly better or slightly worse than other times?\n\nPatient: No, the time of year really doesn't make a difference. Most days there's just this... this low, gray mood running in the background. Like, I can still go to the lab and do my job and talk to people, but I don't really feel light or excited about things anymore. Even stuff I used to love, like baking or checking the telescope, it's just... dull. \n\nSpeaking of the telescope, I actually had to recalibrate the primary mirror on my Dobsonian reflector last weekend. It's an 8-inch aperture, which is decent for amateur planetary observation, but the collimation was completely knocked out of alignment. I suspect it happened when the temperature dropped suddenly on Tuesday. The thermal expansion coefficient of the borosilicate glass mirror is pretty low, but the steel tube housing contracts much faster in the cold, which can warp the tension on the mounting springs. It's a delicate system. You have to use a laser collimator to align the secondary mirror to the center of the primary, and then adjust the primary so the laser reflects perfectly back onto itself. \n\nI was trying to explain the geometry of optical alignment to Dave when he asked what I did over the weekend. I told him about the parabolic curve of the primary mirror and how it focuses incoming light to a single point, and he asked if he could use it to melt ants like a magnifying glass. A magnifying glass! First of all, a telescope uses a concave mirror, not a convex lens, so the focal point is inside the tube. Secondly, the sheer disrespect for precision optical equipment... I mean, the aluminum coating on that mirror is only a few molecules thick. If you even breathe on it wrong, you can degrade the reflectivity. I had to walk away before I explained the physics of thermal energy concentration to him in a way that might have involved his own hand. \n\nBut, um... sorry. What were you asking? Seasons? Right. No, winter or summer, it doesn't matter. It's just always that same gray feeling in the background.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really. I don't sit around feeling guilty about things. I mean, I do my work, I try to keep things running, but I don't have this overwhelming sense of guilt or anything. 

I did feel a little bad the other day when I snapped at the delivery guy, though. He brought in this shipment of anhydrous solvents, and he left the boxes sitting right next to the radiator in the loading bay. The flash point of diethyl ether is minus 45 degrees Celsius. Minus 45! And it has this nasty habit of forming explosive peroxides if it's exposed to light and heat over time. We usually order it with BHT as an inhibitor, but even then, you don't just leave it next to a heat source. The vapor pressure at room temperature is already incredibly high—it boils at 34 degrees. If that radiator kicked on and the ambient temperature spiked, the pressure inside those amber glass bottles would increase exponentially. It's basic gas laws, PV=nRT. If the volume is constant, the pressure has to go up with the temperature. 

I had to grab the spill containment cart and move all six cases into the flammables cabinet myself. And while I was doing that, I realized the grounding wire on the cabinet had come loose from the wall terminal. You have to ground flammables cabinets because the friction of just pouring a non-polar solvent can generate static electricity, and a single spark is all it takes to ignite the vapor. I had to hunt down a wrench to re-secure the grounding clamp, and of course, Dave had borrowed my wrench set last week and returned it with the 10-millimeter socket missing. It is a universal truth of the cosmos that the 10-millimeter socket is always the one that disappears. I swear they evaporate into a parallel dimension. 

But, um... wait. Guilt? Oh. No, I don't really feel guilty about the past or anything. I just... I don't feel much of anything, really.
